CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION This Sixth Semiannual Report of the Small Business 

Administration discusses various aspects of the economic position of small business and describes in detail the activities of the Agency in the three areas assigned to it by Congress: 

1. Helping small business gain access to adequate capital and credit. 

2. Helping small business obtain a fair share of Government procurement. 

3. Helping small business obtain competent management, technical, and production counseling.

The lending activities of the Agency have been of increasing help to small business. During the 6-months period January 1June 30, 1956, the Small Business Administration approved 1, 290 

business loans in a gross amount of $51, 644, 869, to assist small firms, a marked increase over the $30, 332, 390 of business loans approved in the previous period. Both the number of applications for financial assistance and the percentage of approvals increased sharply, while the time required for processing applications was reduced. A notable accomplishment was the inauguration January 1, 1956, of the Limited Loan Participation Program. This new loan plan gives promise of becoming an increasingly effective means of assisting small businesses which might otherwise find credit unavailable. In the area of Government procurement, substantial progress was made in the program under which the Small Business Administration and Government purchasing agencies jointly set aside proposed purchases for award to small business concerns. 1

More than 3, 900 proposed Government purchases, involving an estimated expenditure of $304, 864, 565, were reserved during this period for competitive award to small firms. This was an increase of more than $91, 000, 000 over the value of set-asides during the first 6 months of 1955. Progress of the set-aside program reflects, in part, the effectiveness of the new basic agreement between this Agency and the Department of Defense. 

Co-operative set-aside programs also have begun functioning in purchasing offices of the Armed Services Petroleum Purchasing 

Agency, Veterans Administration, General Services Administration, Department of Agriculture, and the Department of the Interior. 

Establishment of these additional programs indicates that there will be even greater participation by small business in Government purchasing during fiscal year 1957. 

In addition to review of the lending program and the set-aside program, this report discusses progress of the Small Business Administration's many other services to small business in the financial, procurement, management, production and technical fields.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION

CHAPTER II ECONOMIC POSITION OF SMALL BUSINESS 

This chapter discusses various aspects of the economic position of small business. It includes data on such indicators of the health of small business as changes in business population, new business incorporations, business failures, sales and earnings of small as compared with large manufacturing corporations, and the small business share of Government contracts. Various financial factors affecting the health and growth of small business also are discussed.

Business Population 

At the beginning of 1956 there were 4, 252, 000 business concerns in operation in the United States, an increase of more than 60,000 concerns over the business population a year earlier. 

This was a greater increase than in any year since 1948, the last year of the rapid growth of business population which started at the close of World War II, as shown in chart 1, page 4. Since 1948 the annual average number of new businesses started has exceeded the number discontinued by 38, 300.

The rise in number of business concerns was reflected in each of the major industry divisions except manufacturing, which continued to dip slightly. Manufacturing was the only major industry division in which the number of operating concerns increased throughout the World War II period; manufacturing also shared in the rapid rise in the business population in the earlier postwar years. Contract construction showed the largest relative increase in operating concerns last year, 6-1/2 per cent, while trade and service divisions each gained about 1-1/2 per cent. Since 1939 the increase in the business population has kept pace with the increase in the Nation's human population, there being a 28 per cent increase in business population during this period compared to a 27 per cent increase in human population.

New Business Incorporations 

The number of new business incorporations during the first half of 1956 was 76, 257 compared with 74, 357 during the same period a year ago. The 139, 651 new business incorporations in 1955 was a record number. The total for 1956 may be even higher.

Statistics on business incorporations include not only establishment of new businesses but also conversion of existing businesses into corporations. While the increase in new business incorporations beginning with 1954 has been due partly to changes in the tax law of benefit to enterprises which use the corporate form, a primary factor has been the continuing opportunities for a large number of new businesses. 

The monthly totals of new business incorporations during 1955 and the first half of 1956 are shown in chart 2 below. In every month during 1954 the number of incorporations was greater than in the corresponding month of the preceding year; with the exception

The current high level of new business incorporations may be observed by comparing the monthly totals with the monthly averages for the years 1946 through 1955. Data for earlier years are not available.

Business Failures 

Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., reported a total of 6, 496 business failures during the first 6 months of 1956 compared with 5, 626 during the same period of 1955. This is an increase of 15 per cent.

The average number of business failures for the prewar years 1939-41 was 13, 412. These were the last 3 years prior to 

World War II. These years prior to hostilities and the subsequent adjustments provide an acceptable basis for analyzing business failures. As would be expected, the number of business failures during each of the years of World War II, the immediate postwar years, the Korean period, and the year of adjustment following the end of the Korean hostilities was below the current number of failures. The period 1954 to the present time is more nearly comparable to the 1939-41 period. 

The total number of business failures reported for all months of 1955 and the first 6 months of 1956 are shown on an annual basis in chart 3 on page 8. In order to present the current level of business failures in relation to the historical experience of the business economy, the total number of business failures is given for each of the years 1900 through 1955. 

The increase in business failures this year has resulted primarily from the decrease in residential construction, the cutback in the automobile industry, and the more competitive situation which exists in textiles and apparel. Usually, business failures tend to be highest in March of each year and to taper off through the late spring and summer months, with some increase during the latter part of the year. Business failures in the first half of 1956 reached a peak of 1, 170 in March. It is too early to conclude that the usual seasonal pattern will characterize 1956.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION 

Prior to 1933, business failures included agents and commercial services (such as real estate and insurance brokers, holding and finance companies, tourist agencies, etc.) which are not included in later years. Data from 

1939 through June 1956 include more complete coverage of small firms (particularly voluntary discontinuances with loss to creditors and small concerns forced out of business by such actions as attachments). It is useful to look at the rate of business failures. The failure rate used by Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., is equal to the number of failures per 10,000 firms listed in the Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., Reference Book. The failure rates for each of the years 1900-1955 as well as the monthly failure rates for 1955 and the first 6 months of 1956 are indicated on chart 4 above. For the first half of 1956, the failure rate was 48.7 per 10,000 firms. The failure rate for the first half of 1955 was 42. 2. For the years 1939-41 the average was 63.

Annually, Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., makes a classification of the causes of business failures. For the 12 months ended June 30, 1955, it was found that the underlying cause of one-half of the business failures was incompetence. More than 9 out of 10 business failures were due to incompetence plus unbalanced experience, lack of managerial experience, and lack of experience in the line. This points up the necessity for more training, work experience, and counseling before launching a new business. This Agency has been expanding its activities in this area to the fullest extent possible. 

Financial Position of Smaller Manufacturing Corporations 

Smaller manufacturing corporations (those having total assets under $1,000, 000) continue to share in the improvement of business earnings which began in the second quarter of 1954 and continued through the first quarter of 1956. This upward trend in earnings is visible in various indexes, including net sales, earnings before and after taxes, and return on stockholders' equity. 

Nevertheless, there remains a substantial gap between the level of earnings of the smaller and larger manufacturing corporations. This is due primarily to the lower rate of earnings and the slower growth of some industries such as food and apparel compared with the higher rate of earnings and the rapid growth of other industries such as aircraft.

Net Sales 

During the first quarter of 1956 the net sales of both smaller and larger manufacturing corporations exceeded the volume of business done in the first quarter of 1955. Net sales of the smaller manufacturing corporations increased from $8, 299 million to $8,767 million (5. 6 per cent) while net sales of the larger manufacturing corporations increased from $57, 341 million to $63, 112 million (10. 1 per cent). During the four quarters ending with the first quarter of 1956 net sales of smaller corporations increased to $36, 698 million from $34, 217 million in the preceding four quarters (7.3 per cent).

The base of 100 for the indexes used below to indicate financial position is the period 1947-49. The index numbers were computed from the annual data contained in Statistics of Income published by the Internal Revenue Service of the U. S. Treasury 

Department and the Quarterly Financial Report for Manufacturing

The trend of net sales during the period 1950-52 was up for both the smaller and the larger manufacturing corporations. 

The index of sales for the smaller manufacturing corporations increased from an average of 104 in 1950 to 117 in the fourth quarter of 1952; at the same time, the index of sales of the larger manufacturing corporations increased from 120 to 151. The index of sales of smaller manufacturing corporations reached a low point of 94 in the first quarter of 1954, but had risen to 99 by the first quarter of 1955. In the first quarter of 1956, the index of sales for the smaller manufacturing corporations was 104 and for the larger manufacturing corporations 162, as shown in chart 5 above. 

Earnings Before Taxes 

During the first quarter of 1956 earnings before taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations totaled $347 million compared with $298 million in the first quarter of 1955. The

smaller manufacturing corporations total earnings before taxes for the four quarters ending with the first quarter of 1956 was $1, 384 million compared with $1, 116 million in the four preceding quarters. 

In spite of the upward trend of earnings before taxes beginning in 1954, the index for the smaller manufacturing corporations was only 83 in the first quarter of 1956. The continued upward trend of earnings before taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations will depend partly on increasing personal consumption expenditures for those goods which are manufactured in substantial quantities by the smaller manufacturers. The smaller manufacturers tend to be concentrated in such industry divisions as food, apparel, lumber, and printing and publishing. Also, higher earnings will depend on other factors such as costs of raw materials and labor. 

Earnings After Taxes

Smaller manufacturing corporations' earnings after taxes, like their earnings before taxes, show an upward trend. During the first quarter of 1956, earnings after taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations totaled $188 million compared with $144 million in the same quarter of 1955. For the four quarters ending with first quarter 1956, smaller manufacturing corporations' earnings after taxes totaled $725 million -- substantially greater than their $559 million of earnings in the preceding four quarters.

The trends of earnings before and after taxes, by asset size of corporations, are presented in chart 6, page 13. 

Earnings before taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations reached a peak in the first quarter of 1951. The index then followed a continuous downward trend through the first quarter of 1954. During the first quarter of 1951, the index of earnings was 

133. The base period years of 1947-49≈ 100. In the first quarter of 1954, the index was only 60. Since the first quarter of 1954, there has been a steady upward trend to an index of 82. 

The index of smaller manufacturing corporations' earnings after taxes was 125 during the first quarter of 1951 but declined sharply during the first year and a half of the Korean conflict and again in 1953 when military contracts were being canceled as a result of the ending of hostilities. During the first quarter of 1954, the index of earnings was only 46. However, as a result of a sharp improvement in earnings during the past 2 years, the index has risen to 77.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION

Earnings on Stockholders' Equity 

Earnings on stockholders' equity offer another comparison of earnings of large and small manufacturing corporations. Stockholders' equity is composed of the amounts of capital stock outstanding, earned surplus and undivided profits, surplus reserves and that portion of other reserves which is considered surplus. 

In 1947 the annual rate of earnings before taxes on stockholders' equity of smaller manufacturing corporations (those with assets under $1 million) was 27.8 per cent. After a sharp drop to 

12.9 per cent in 1949 it increased to 21. 8 per cent in 1950. However, by 1952 it had declined to 16.9 per cent. This downward trend which took place during and immediately following the Korean period was halted by the first quarter of 1954. For the four quarters ending with the first quarter of 1956, the rate had risen to 15. 4 per cent from the low point of 10.4 per cent 2 years ago.

The trend of the annual rate of earnings after taxes of small manufacturing corporations was similar: A decline from 16. 3 per cent in 1947 to 7.0 per cent in 1949; an increase to 12. 5 per cent in 1950 followed by a downward trend to 4.9 per cent after Korea and a rise to 7.5 per cent for the four quarters ending with the first quarter of 1956, as shown in chart 7 on page 15. 

Small Manufacturing Corporations' Share of Total Sales and Earnings 

The small business share of total sales and earnings of manufacturing corporations has declined. From 18.9 per cent of sales in 1947, the small business percentage of sales had dropped to 15. 5 by 1952, 13. 7 by 1953, 13.6 by 1954 and 13. 0 per cent by 

1955. Most of this decrease in the small business share was during the Korean period. The trend, however, is not a source of encouragement.

Earnings before Federal income taxes of small manufacturing corporations dropped from 13. 8 per cent of total earnings of all manufacturing corporations in 1947 to 7. 3 per cent in both 

1951 and 1952. In 1953 they were 5. 6 per cent, in 1954, 4. 8 per cent and in 1955, 4.7 per cent.

The trend of earnings after taxes was similar. The proportion of earnings after taxes going to the smaller manufacturing corporations decreased from 13. 3 per cent of total corporate

2 manufacturing earnings after taxes in 1947 to 7. 0 per cent in 1949.

Following an increase to 9. 3 per cent in 1950, there was a rather continuous downward trend until 1954, when earnings of the smaller manufacturing corporations were 4. 3 per cent of the total. In 1955 they were 4. 5 per cent.

This decline in the relative proportion of total sales and earnings going to smaller manufacturing corporations has resulted primarily from the fact that the aircraft, motor vehicle, and chemicals industries grew more rapidly than such industries as food and apparel, which include large numbers of small firms. 

Smaller manufacturing corporations tend to be concentrated more in industries whose growth is more nearly like that of the human population and whose earnings characteristically have been lower than those of the aircraft, motor vehicle, and chemicals industries. 

Military Procurement Amount of Awards to Small Business Increases 

The total value of net military procurement under prime contracts awarded during the period July 1955 through April 1956 was $12,025 million. The small business share was $2, 486 million, or 20.7 per cent. During the same months of the preceding fiscal year, the total amount was $10, 642 million of which the small business share was $2,268 million, or 21. 3 per cent. Net military procurement is equal to new military procurement (new commitments of funds) under prime contracts awarded, less cancellations during the same period of prior contract commitments. 

During July through April of fiscal year 1956, new military procurement commitments totaled $14, 142 million. The small business share was $2, 552 million, or 18. 0 per cent. During the same months of the preceding fiscal year, the total was $13, 314 million of which the small business share was $2, 351 million, or 17. 7 per cent. The table on page 18 shows the totals for both new and net military procurement for the months of July through April of fiscal year 1955 compared with the same months of fiscal year 1956. This table includes the amount of the small business share of procurement by each military service and by the Armed Services Petroleum Purchasing Agency. The table on page 19 gives the percentage distribution of total military procurement by size of business and by each military service.

The Army small business share of total new procurement decreased from 10.0 per cent in the July April period of fiscal year 1955 to 8.7 per cent for the same period in fiscal year 

1956; the Navy share increased from 4. 3 to 5. 0 per cent; the Air 

Force from 3. 0 to 3. 2 per cent; and the Armed Services Petroleum 

Purchasing Agency from 0.4 to 1. 1 per cent. As a result, there was a slight increase in the over-all small business percentage of new military procurement from 17.7 to 18. 0 per cent. 

Small business concerns make an additional significant contribution to Government purchasing through subcontracts awarded to them by prime contractors. Unfortunately, adequate statistics to measure this participation are not yet available. However, the 

Department of Defense and the Small Business Administration have been working to strengthen their subcontracting assistance programs. 

The Department of Defense has established reporting procedures which should result in a better appraisal of the total small business share of military procurement. 

The Department of Defense has made a study of the effect of the advertising, negotiation and publishing synopses of bids and awards on small business participation in defense procurement. 

This study covers the period from July to October 1955, inclusive. 

On the basis of this study, the Assistant Secretary of Defense (Supply and Logistics) has pointed out that if items not in the small business potential are excluded from consideration, small business does about as well in military procurement by negotiation as by advertising. The small business potential of negotiated procurement is not relatively as great as for advertised procurement. The Small 

Business Administration understands that the Department of Defense will make a new study of this subject when data for the entire fiscal year 1956 are available.

During the period July 1955 through April 1956, both the amount and the percentage of advertised new procurement, as against negotiated new procurement, increased. The amount increased to $2, 052 million from $1, 681 million during July 1954 through April 1955. The percentage increased from 12. 6 per cent to 14. 5 per cent of total military purchasing. The small business share of advertised new procurement increased while its share of negotiated procurement decreased. On a net basis, there were similar changes.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION

Small Business Share, Fiscal Years 1951-56 

After Korea the total value of net military procurement decreased but the percentage of awards to small business increased. 

The small business percentage for the period July 1953 through April 1956 was 22. 4 compared with 18. 1 during the preceding 3 fiscal years.

This higher small business percentage indicates that the combined efforts of the Department of Defense and the Small Business Administration have achieved results. 

The net value of military prime contracts with small business reached a peak of $7, 066 million in fiscal year 1952. By fiscal year 1954 it had decreased to $2,902 million. In fiscal year 

1955 it rose to $3, 214 million. It is estimated that the small business total was higher in fiscal year 1956. The year-to-year changes of both the net value of military prime contracts with small and large business and the small business percentages are shown in chart 8 on page 21.

Changes in military requirements during fiscal years 1951 through 1956 caused significant changes in the distribution of

procurement by military service. The Air Force percentage of the military procurement dollar increased from less than 30 per cent in fiscal years 1951 and 1952 to between 40 and 50 per cent in the subsequent years. There also was an increase in the percentage of total procurement by the Armed Services Petroleum Purchasing Agency. The Navy percentage of the procurement dollar was relatively stable, varying within a range of 23 to 32 per cent. However, the Army share of the military procurement dollar has decreased from nearly half in fiscal year 1951 to less than one-fourth since Korea, as shown in chart 9 on page 22. 

The small business potential for supplying the goods and services required by the different military services varies widely. As shown in chart 10 below, the small business percentage of Army procurement has been much higher than in the case of Navy or Air Force procurement. Also, it is lower for the 

Armed Services Petroleum Purchasing Agency. Both the small business potential and percentage are lower for the Air Force. 

Consequently, a decrease in the over-all small business percentage of military procurement was expected. The fact that the small business percentage of military prime contract awards actually has been higher since Korea is noteworthy. Even so, this points up the necessity of increasing the effectiveness of the subcontracting assistance programs of the 

Department of

Small Business Administration.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION

undoubtedly have been benefited by the procurement assistance programs of both the Department of Defense and the Small Business Administration, the small business share of military prime contracts is not equal to the small business potential. According to the Department of Defense, during the period July 1955 through April 1956, contracts awarded to small business amounted to 61.9 per cent of the total small business potential of the total net value of military procurement actions with small and large business firms for work in the United States. An analysis of the reasons for small business failure to obtain potential military procurement prime contract awards of $10,000 or more indicated that the small business bid was not low on procurement actions having a value equal to 32.4 per cent of the small business potential of awards of $10,000 or more. On other procurement actions having a value equal to 5. 5 per cent of the potential, there was no bid from a small business. 

These two reasons accounted for 96.3 per cent of the potential of awards of $10,000 or more that were not won by small business. 

There have been a number of complaints that large business enterprises anxious to diversify their product lines bid below cost on items being procured by the military service. This is an advantage which the large concern may have over the small business which cannot afford to take a loss on a new product line even though it might offer good profit possibilities for the long run. 

There has been an upward trend in the amount of military procurement set aside for small business through joint action of the Department of Defense and the Small Business Administration. 

The total amount set aside for small business increased from $248 million in fiscal year 1954 to $387 million in fiscal year 1955, to $498 million in 1956. The recent sharp upward trend is shown in chart 11 on page 25. The trend of procurement set aside for small business is shown in relation to both total military contract awards and contracts awarded to small business. The increase in set-asides has resulted from extending this program to more procurement installations, changes in the set-aside program which were made in November 1955, and better co-operation between the agencies.

Atomic Energy Commission 

The Atomic Energy Commission small business procurement assistance program is centered on subcontracting. The small business percentage of subcontract dollars increased sharply from 26.7 per cent in fiscal year 1951 to 45. 6 per cent in fiscal year 1955. 

It was 46.9 per cent for the first three quarters of fiscal year 1956. 

Because of the decline in total subcontract dollars from $783. 2 million in fiscal year 1953 to $318. 5 million during the first three quarters of fiscal year 1956, there was a decline in the amount of subcontracts awarded to small business from $311. 6 million to $149. 3 million. 

In addition to the subcontracts of $149. 3 million, small business received prime contract awards totaling $23.6 million.

Financing Problems 

It has been stated that the tightening of credit by the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, which occurred during this period, had a heavier impact on small business than on larger borrowers. Mr. Allan Sproul, formerly president of the

Federal Reserve Bank of New York, speaking in May 1956 on the effect of the credit restraints, stated that "I suspect that the impact of a policy of credit restraint is greater on 'small business' than on big business. . . it seems fairly clear that 'small business' has less access to alternative sources of funds when bank credit is not readily available and only less clear that its access to bank credit is likely to be more difficult under such circumstances. Access to bank credit is achieved primarily by reason of the credit worthiness of the borrower, long established customer relationships with the lender, and the profitability of the transaction. It may well be that big business has an edge in these respects, which becomes apparent when not everyone can get all of the credit that everyone wants. 11 During the first 6 months of 1956, demands for bank credit increased and bank loans rose sharply. The increased demand is attributable to the record expenditures by industry for plant and equipment, accelerated tax payments, some increase in credit to consumers and the higher cost of doing business due to the need of carrying heavier inventories and meeting higher material and payroll costs. The restraint on credit by the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, which was later partially relaxed, did not halt the rise in the level of bank loans. Small business suffered not only from credit restraint in meeting its needs, but also because the filling up of bank portfolios with various types of loans made some banks turn away many would-be small business borrowers. Retained profits have been a prime source of capital; in 

1955 undistributed profits amounted to 46. 9 per cent of total corporate profits after taxes compared with 39. 0 per cent in 1954; and in 

1956 it is indicated that retained profits will remain at approximately the 1955 level. On the whole, small businesses can rely much less on plowing back of internal profits than large businesses and are in a less favorable position to tap this source of capital. On October 5, 1955, the Federal Reserve Board made a survey of loans made by member banks. A comparison between the survey made in 1955 and one made in 1946 revealed that over the postwar years a substantial shift occurred in the distribution of loans 398407 O 56 - 3

by asset size of borrower. The proportion of loans to concerns with assets under $50, 000 declined considerably; loans to concerns with assets of between $50, 000 and $250, 000 rose to a higher percentage of the total number even though they declined in percentage of total amount. The percentage of loans to firms with assets of between $250,000 and $5, 000, 000 rose both in number and amount, while loans to borrowers with assets over $5, 000, 000 remained in approximately the same proportion.

According to the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System the shift in the distribution of outstanding loans from the smallest to the intermediate categories between 1946 and 1955 probably reflects in large part the general upward valuation of business assets and the growth in total business volume while the asset size classes remain unchanged. The shift may also reflect the unusually large proportion of new and re-established small businesses in the immediate postwar business population and the increasing availability of internal and nonbank funds as business firms mature and become established. Increased reliance on internal and nonbank funds may also explain in part the absence of any appreciable rise in the proportion of loans to enterprises with assets of $5, 000, 000 and over. Whatever the rationalization, the survey does reveal a decline in the proportion of the amount of loans made to firms with under $250,000 in assets.

Equity Capital for Small Business 

Total corporate securities offerings during the period January through May 1956 were not as high as during the same months of 1955. The decrease in the total was from $4, 175 million to $4, 149 million. However, the decrease in offerings of equity securities (common and preferred stocks) was much greater $1,375 million to $982 million. There was a similar decline in small offerings between $100 thousand and $300 thous and from $109 million to $86 million.

In addition to the total of 321 mergers in the metals and metalworking industries, there were 87 mergers in the food industries and 52 in the chemicals industries. Although all other manufacturing industries include nearly two-thirds of the total manufacturing population, in these there were only 166 mergers, or approximately one-fourth of the total mergers in manufacturing. 

In the wholesale and retail trades there were 83 mergers-less than 10 per cent of the corporate mergers, despite the fact that wholesalers and retailers comprise one-half the total business population. Similarly, corporate mergers were relatively low in the services and miscellaneous industries. In this category, which includes a little over 40 per cent of the total business population, there were only 47 mergers (5. 6 per cent of all mergers).

A number of corporate mergers among smaller businesses were not tabulated by the Federal Trade Commission. Nevertheless, it is apparent that corporate mergers have a far greater effect in certain manufacturing industries than in other industry divisions.

CHAPTER III FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE Activity in the Small Business Administration's Business 

Loan Program increased greatly during the period January 1 through June 30, 1956. The Agency approved 1, 290 business loans in a total amount of $51, 644, 868, to assist small firms. Demand for disaster loans was not as great as in the previous 6-months period, but the Agency approved 1, 253 such loans aggregating $15, 111, 872, to assist businesses and home owners. This record was made although during this period the Agency had to cease approving any business loans because its lending funds were exhausted. An additional appropriation of $20,000,000 was granted to replenish the fund in May 1956 and the lending activity resumed.

During the previous 6-months period, business loan approvals totaled $30, 332, 390 and disaster loan approvals $29, 290, 391. 

Loan approvals during the fiscal year which ended June 30, 1956, totaled 5, 224 for $126, 379, 521, as compared with 2,258 approvals for $63, 847, 227 during the previous fiscal year. 

The sharp increase in lending activities of the Agency during the past fiscal year is attributable to the following principal factors: 

1. Tightening of the private credit available to small concerns; 

2. Improvement in the quality of business loan applications as indicated by approval of nearly 57 per cent of total applications acted on during the year, compared with approval of about 37 per cent on a cumulative basis through the end of the previous year; 35

3. Introduction of the Agency's Limited Loan 

Participation Plan under which term financing has been made available to many small businesses previously unable to qualify for loans; 4. 5. 1. 

Wider general knowledge of the lending program of the Small Business Administration resulting from increased attention to the Agency's activities by the daily and business press, trade associations and other business groups; and

Loans approved by the Small Business Administration from the beginning of its financial assistance program in October 1953 through June 30, 1956, totaled 8, 112 for $218, 707, 621. Of this total, 3, 560 for $165, 690, 771 were business loans, and 4, 552 for $53,016, 850 were disaster loans. 2. 

Increased disaster rehabilitation activity resulting from natural catastrophes, particularly the storms and floods which ravaged the Northeastern States late last summer and fall, and the floods which occurred on the West Coast last winter.

During the last 6-months period, the Congress approved a supplemental appropriation of $20 million to the revolving fund for lending purposes. An additional $50 million has been similarly approved for fiscal year 1957. Appropriations to the revolving fund now total $175 million. However, loan approvals against the fund may exceed the total appropriation for the following reasons: 

Loan approvals include the banks' share of participation loans. In the case of deferred participation loans, the Small 

Business Administration's fund commit ment is for only that portion of the total which it is estimated will be required to purchase at some later date. 

At the borrowers' requests prior to disbursement, some approved loans have been canceled. A frequent reason

3. 4. 

Financial Counseling for cancellation is that the small firm has been able to obtain bank financing as a result of the work of the Small Business Administration in developing a sound basis for a loan to the firm. 

Loan repayments have now reached sizeable amounts. These funds are available for use in making additional loans.

The Agency offers its seasoned direct loans for sale to private lending institutions. Funds realized in this way are also used to replenish the revolving fund.

The increased demand for financial assistance, which is indicated by the number of business loan applications filed, has also been reflected in the number of loan inquiries received by the Agency. Most of these are personal inquiries by businessmen visiting the Agency's regional offices. 

After reviewing information on the small firm's operations, financial condition, and qualifications for financing, Small Business Administration staff members often find that a loan is not the solution to the businessman's problem. In other cases, where borrowing is the solution, Agency specialists frequently help the businessman obtain the necessary funds from private sources. 

Many inquirers merely seek general information about the Agency's loan program as a possible source of future financing. 

Others frequently cannot qualify for loans because of inadequate earnings prospects, lack of collateral, or the nature of their business. In every instance, however, the Small Business Administration's financial specialists try to help the small business owner through counseling. Often a small firm's problem is under capitalization, or perhaps a need for liquidation of inventory or replacement of obsolete equipment, revision of credit policies or a change in product pricing principles. In other instances, a contemplated expansion may not be justified, the firm's product line may be

Loan Applications 

During this period, the Small

Business Administration received 2, 578 business loan applications. This compares with 1, 499 applications received during the similar 

6-months period of 1955.

The increased demand for business loans is indicated in chart 13, which shows the number of applications received during each month of fiscal years 1955 and 1956. During the period January-June 1956, the number of applications reached a high of 514 in the month of June and ranged from 267 to 505 in the other months. During the 

6-months period covered in the previous semiannual report (July 1 - December 31, 1955), applications reached a high of 280 in September and were between 205 and 273 in the other months of the period.

JUL AUG SEPT OCT NOV DEC JAN FEB MAR APR MAY JUN SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION 

The Small Business Administration has reduced the time required to take action on loan applications in the following manner: 

1. Emphasis has been placed upon speeding up the processing of each application, including preparation of the financial specialist's report. Within no longer than a 21-day period, visits to the applicant's place of business, contacts with the applicant's bank

2. of deposit and development of additional credit data where necessary, must be made. 

Regional directors have been given increased authority to take final action on loan applications. They may now approve direct loans in amounts not to exceed $20, 000 and participation loans in amounts not to exceed $50,000 where the participating institution's share of the loan is not less than 25 per cent of the total loan. 

3. Managers of larger branch offices have been authorized to approve loans made under the Limited Loan Participation Program. 

This action has brought the Small Business Administration's financial assistance closer to many firms and reduced the time consumed in processing applications. 

Bank Participations 

Largely due to the new Limited Loan Participation

Program, loans approved during this 6-months period included a greater proportion of participation loans than in any previous period. About 73 per cent of the loans approved during January through June 1956 were in participation with banks. 

A bank participation loan provides the small business borrower the opportunity of building up a satisfactory credit record with the bank. After the loan is repaid, the bank then has a firmer basis for extending necessary credit to the small firm without enlisting the aid of the Government. Thus, the bank participationtype loan is of broader assistance to the small business than a direct Government loan. Of interest to the taxpayer is the fact that in a majority of bank participation loans, the Agency participation is on a deferred basis and the Government funds committed probably never will be withdrawn from the Treasury. In contrast, immediate participation and direct loans require immediate disbursement of 

Federal funds.

The increase in the proportion of participation loans and corresponding decrease in the number of direct loans were

Limited Loan Participation Program 

This program was designed especially to assist small retailers, wholesalers and service establishments to finance modernization and expansion of their operations. Many small businesses of this type have competent management, good earnings records, and a creditable record with local banks for meeting obligations, but have very little tangible collateral which they can pledge as security for loans.

An industry analysis of the loans approved under this program is as follows:

Under this loan plan the Small Business Administration will participate with a bank in a loan to a small firm up to a maximum of $15,000, or 75 per cent of the loan, whichever is the lesser. The bank share must represent exposure on the part of the bank of not less than 25 per cent of the total amount of the loan. 

Maximum maturity is 5 years. Repayment is monthly. The maximum interest rate is 6 per cent per annum.

An application for this type of loan is made directly to a bank on Small Business Administration application form. The 

Agency takes final action based upon the bank's recommendation and the data submitted. 

This program was operated on a trial basis during the last few months of 1955, and was placed in operation on a nationwide basis on January 1, 1956. From January 1 through June 30, 1956, 472 loans for $6, 775, 490 were approved. Total loans under the program, as of June 30, were 519 for $7, 310, 205.

Approximately 69 per cent of these loans were to retail, wholesale and service establishments as compared with 39. 2 per cent of all Agency business loans to these industries on a cumulative basis.

Approvals and Declines 

A report of business loan application activity from the start of the Agency's lending program in October 1953 through June 30, 1956, is given on page 44. The total number of applications received was 9,688. Of this number, 3, 560 were approved 

1,299 by regional offices and 2, 261 by the Washington office.

One thousand, three hundred and seventy applications were withdrawn before final action was taken thereon. In a large number of cases where applications were withdrawn, financing had been obtained from private sources through the assistance of the Agency's regional financial specialists. A total of 4, 275 applications were declined and 483 were pending as of June 30, 1956. -0 Through June 30, 1956, about 45 per cent of the loan applications on which final action had been taken were approved. 

During this reporting period, more than 60 per cent of the applications processed were approved. Approval ratios have increased sharply during each of the last four 6-months periods, as indicated in chart 15 on page 43. Where a loan application does not appear to present a reasonable credit risk, the Agency seeks to improve the basis of the application so that it will merit favorable action. However, many applications fall short of prudent credit standards, and it would be a disservice to both the Nation and small business to make loans on an unsound basis.

JAN-JUN 1956 SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION Loans to Proprietary Hospitals and Nursing Homes 

The Small Business Administration has extended its lending program to make loans to proprietary hospitals and nursing homes operated for profit. In this respect it is supplementing the program of the Department of Health, Education, and Welfare, under the Hill-Burton Act, in providing needed community health facilities or in upgrading such facilities. 

Funds provided under the Hill-Burton Act are made available in co-operation with Federal, State and local officials for the construction of new hospitals and nursing homes sponsored either by a nonprofit organization or by a city, county or State. 

Loans made by the Small Business Administration are confined to privately owned health facilities which are not eligible for assistance under the Hill-Burton Act. Agency officials have been working with representatives of the Department of Health, Education, and Welfare in the establishment of criteria for the consideration of such loans and to fit the program into that Department's priority system.

Section 207(b) of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended, gives the Small Business Administration authority to make loans to assist in the rehabilitation of homes and businesses which have been damaged or destroyed by floods, storms or other natural disasters. The Agency is also authorized to make loans to businesses within an area where a drought is occurring and which have suffered economic loss as a result of the drought. These loans are limited to businesses within an area designated as a drought disaster area by the President or the Secretary of Agriculture. 

During the last 6-months period, the Congress approved an increase of $100 million to a total of $125 million, in the statutory limitation on the maximum amount of disaster loans outstanding at any one time.

The number of disaster loan applications filed during this reporting period was not nearly as great as in the previous 

6-months period.

On the other hand, loans of over $150, 000 in size have more than doubled in terms of proportions both in number of loans and total amount approved during the past year. 

This reflects the 1955 amendment to the Small Business Act which increased the Agency's maximum share in a business loan from $150, 000 to $250,000 and gave the Agency authority to make loans to corporations formed and capitalized by groups of small business concerns for the purpose of establishing facilities to produce or secure raw materials or supplies.

After the Small Business Administration approves a loan to a small business concern, a formal loan authorization is prepared and delivered to the applicant. If a bank is participating in the loan, a copy of the loan authorization is also sent to the bank. The authorization states the amount of the approved loan; the collateral to be pledged; such general terms as rates of repayment required and final maturity date; and such standard provisions as are deemed necessary to assure proper safeguards for the taxpayers' money. It then becomes the applicant's obligation to take the necessary steps to fulfill the terms and conditions of the loan. 

Until this has been done, the Agency cannot disburse the funds. 

The borrower is customarily allowed 4 months in which to meet these requirements but may request an extension.

Frequently borrowers are responsible for delays in the disbursement of approved loans. These delays may be due to difficulties in starting construction of facilities; problems in acquisition of machinery and equipment; flaws in real estate titles; deaths of key employees; adverse changes in borrowers' financial condition and other reasons. 

The Small Business Administration has devoted considerable study to speeding up its part in the disbursement of approved loans. During the last 6 months, special attention has been given to the streamlining of procedures, the reduction in number and content of required forms, redelegation of authority, and closer follow-up with borrowers to assist them in meeting terms and obligations.

As of June 30, 1956, disbursements had been made on 2, 220 of the 2, 950 net approved business loans -- slightly over 75 per cent of net approvals. Disbursement has not been made on 730 business loans.

As of June 30, 1956, disbursements made by banks or the Small Business Administration on business loans amounted to $100, 640, 149, out of total net commitments to borrowers of $136, 014, 649. The Small Business Administration's share of this net commitment amount was $113, 011, 151. In the case of disaster loans, net commitments to borrowers were $47, 080, 723. The Small Business Administration share was $45, 226, 278. Disbursements totaled $38, 487, 282. 

Consequently, the total amount of net commitments to all borrowers was $183, 095, 372, of which the Small Business Administration share was $158, 237, 429.

Sale of Loans 

More than a year ago, a program was initiated by the 

Agency to sell its seasoned loans. To date, 40 loans amounting to $2, 117, 700, have been sold to banks or refinanced through them. No loan is offered for sale unless it has been disbursed for a 6-months period or longer. In all cases the borrower's bank of deposit is initially contacted to see if it is interested in purchasing the loan. This is in line with the Small Business 

Administration's policy of trying to help the small business concern establish a normal bank-customer relationship. Since this is a

continuing program, the Agency is presently offering through its regional offices all of its seasoned business loans for sale to private lending institutions. Therefore, there can be no question but that the Agency is complying with the Congressional intent that it should not compete with private lending institutions. 

Cancellations 

Of the 3, 560 business loans approved since the start of the Small Business Administration, 610 have been canceled in full prior to disbursement. The principal reasons for cancellation have been: 1. 

2. Borrower determined he could get along without the loan; 4. 

Borrower made other arrangements for funds; 

Borrower was unable or unwilling to comply with loan conditions;

Borrower's financial condition deteriorated to the extent that the loan could not be disbursed.

Delinquencies and Charge-Offs 

As previously stated, disbursement has been made on 

2, 220 business loans in a total amount of $100, 640, 149. Of these 

46 loans are in a liquidation status and 41 have payments due which are delinquent in excess of 60 days. On these 87 loans the unpaid principal balance is $3, 590, 296, and the amount thereof delinquent (including the full unpaid principal on "liquidation" loans) is $2, 236, 309. In addition, liquidation actions have resulted in the charge-off of $70, 224 of principal on three loans. With respect to the total disbursements of $38, 487, 282 on 3, 407 disaster loans, borrowers were delinquent in excess of 60 days on 123 loans with an unpaid principal of $534, 658. The amount delinquent was $144, 364. 81. Only one loan has been charged off, with a principal amount involved of $434.

GOVERNMENT PROCUREMENT CHAPTER IV 

During the 6-months period which ended June 30, 1956, the Small Business Administration made notable progress in its service of assisting small business concerns to participate more fully in the Government's purchases. Of particular significance was a strengthening and expansion of the program under which the 

Small Business Administration and Federal purchasing agencies jointly determine that certain Government purchases should be set aside for award exclusively to small firms on a competitive bid basis.

Highlights of the progress during this period are: 1. 2. 

Through joint action by representatives of the Small Business Administration and the major Federal purchasing agencies, more than 3,900 proposed Government purchases involving an estimated expenditure of $304, 864, 565 were set aside; i. e., reserved for award to small business concerns. This was an increase of almost $91, 000, 000 over the small business set-asides during the first 6 months of 1955.

As a follow-up to policy agreements with other major Government purchasing agencies, Small Business Administration representa tives and buying officials began joint screening of proposed purchases and set asides for small business in the Armed Services 

Petroleum Purchasing Agency, in the Veterans

Administration, in field offices and the National 

Buying Division of the General Services Ad ministration, and in field offices of the 

Department of Agriculture. 57

3. 4. 5. 6. 7.

An agreement was reached with the Department of the Interior providing that Small 

Business Administration and Department of the Interior representatives should jointly review proposed Department of the Interior purchases and reserve suitable ones for award to small firms.

Small Business Administration representa tives began screening proposed purchases at 44 additional Government purchasing offices in order that suitable purchases might be reserved for competitive award to small firms.

The Department of Defense Instruction of 

November 14, 1955 (DOD Directive 4100. 9), which strengthened and expanded the Depart ment of Defense-Small Business Adminis tration program of reserving suitable military purchases for award to small business, has now been implemented by the Army, Navy, and Air Force, each of which has issued necessary operating instructions to guide their purchasing centers. 

More than 36, 000 notifications of prime contract opportunities were sent to small firms by the Small Business Administration, and these referrals resulted in known contract awards totaling $176, 658, 000. The actual awards total is believed to be consider ably greater, since small firms do not always inform the Agency when they receive contracts as a result of the Agency's referrals. 

Approximately 1, 800 notifications of subcontracting opportunities were sent to small firms by Small Business Administration field offices during this 6-months period. In order to develop these opportunities, the Agency staff members made 2, 006 contacts with prime contractors and large subcontractors.

9.

Small Business Administration field offices and representatives at Government buying centers reported 11, 945 cases of counseling small firms on how to sell to the Government or obtain subcontracts, 807 cases of assisting them with problems related to Government purchasing, and 3, 231 cases of obtaining the opportunity for them to bid on current purchases. 

The Small Business Administration furnished 

Government contracting officers the names of 5, 188 small firms which could supply items for which additional small business competition was needed. 

Joint Set-Aside Program for Small Business 

The purpose of this program is to help small business concerns sell to the Government by reserving for competitive bidding or negotiation among small firms all or part of individual Government purchases. To carry out this program, the Small Business Administration has assigned representatives to certain major Government purchasing offices. These representatives screen proposed purchases and advise procurement officials of those which they believe should be earmarked, in whole or in part, for award exclusively to small firms. Each request that all or part of a procurement be reserved for small business is either agreed to, modified or rejected by the procurement officials.

It is still too early to judge conclusively the effectiveness of the new basic agreement for the Small Business Administration- 

Department of Defense Joint Set-Aside Program for Small Business. 

However, during May procurements totaling $64, 011, 809 were jointly restricted to small business concerns, the largest single monthly total since the beginning of the Small Business Administration. The amount restricted during June was still higher $82, 365, 599. This would seem to indicate a definite improvement in the program. 

It is significant that the increase of more than $111, 000, 000 in the amount set aside for small business during fiscal year 1956 was achieved in the face of a procurement decline by two of the three military services. Only the Air Force increased the amount purchased on a new procurement basis. Since the percentage of Air Force procurement susceptible to small business is much lower than that of

the other services, the large increase in the amount restricted to small firms against this adverse trend is especially encouraging. 

The number and value of Government purchases reserved for competitive award to small business concerns has increased steadily during fiscal years 1954, 1955 and 1956. The number of procurements set aside for small business increased from 1, 675 in fiscal year 1954 to 3, 924 in fiscal year 1955 and to 6, 075 in fiscal year 1956. Similarly, the dollar volume of purchases restricted to small firms increased from $248, 000, 000 in fiscal year 1954 to $387, 000, 000 in fiscal year 1955 and to $498, 000, 000 in fiscal year 1956.

Small Business Administration regional offices periodi cally evaluate purchasing being done by the Government offices in their areas to determine the feasibility of expanding the set-aside program to those buying centers not yet covered. During the last 

6 months the program has been extended to 44 additional military and civilian agency purchasing installations. Broader coverage of procurement centers by Small Business Administration representatives means a greater opportunity for small firms to bid on contracts which are suitable to their production and a chance to compete in fields formerly supplied principally by large businesses. 

A list of the 87 military and Federal civilian agency purchasing offices at which joint set-asides for small business were made during the last half of fiscal year 1956 is shown in appendix B.

Joint Set-Aside Program with Military Offices The scope of the Small Business Administration - Department of Defense Joint Set-Aside Program for Small Business has been expanded by the November 14, 1955, DOD Instruction No. 

4100. 9. This policy instruction permits the Small Business Administration to screen for possible restriction to small business concerns the following types of purchases which were previously excluded from the program: Classified procurements, purchases under $10,000, research and development projects, and purchases of an emergency nature. The new instruction also strengthened the joint program in other ways as well.

The Department of the Navy issued instructions implementing the new program on February 2, 1956. In addition, the Navy established a task force to visit its procurement installations to explain and emphasize the new instructions. Army Regulation No. 

715-3, dated February 29, 1956, implementing the Department of

Defense instruction, became effective on issuance. However, it was necessary for the various Technical Services to issue individual operating instructions before the regulations were effective at the contracting officer level. Final implementation was effective during the first part of May. Air Force implementation instructions were issued in May, effective that month. The joint set-asides for small business by military service and by civilian agencies are shown on page 64 for the second half of fiscal year 1956. 1
