Joint Set-Aside Program with Federal Civilian Agencies 

During this fiscal year the Joint Set-Aside Program for Small Business has been established with the following civilian agencies of the Government: 

In September 1955 a pilot set-aside program was established with the New York Branch of the General Services 

Administration's National Buying Division. On November 17, 1955,

a formal policy agreement between the two agencies was signed and in early January the set-aside program was extended to the 

Washington Branch of the National Buying Division. Final imple menting instructions were issued on May 18, establishing a setaside program with the General Services Administration's Federal 

Supply Service throughout all General Services Administration regional offices. The program has now been implemented in all of these offices. By the end of the fiscal year, 150 General Services 

Administration procurements, totaling $6, 350, 928, had been restricted to small firms. 

Veterans Administration 

A policy agreement was signed with the Veterans Administration on January 11, 1956, and a joint set-aside program established soon after in Washington. Sixty-three procurements, totaling $1, 445, 735, had been restricted to small business concerns through June 30.

Department of Agriculture 

The Department of Agriculture and the Small Business Administration adopted a policy and operating agreement for a setaside program on February 7, 1956. Since the Department's buying is decentralized, the Small Business Administration has assigned representatives on a part-time basis to those Department of Agriculture field offices where volume of purchasing warrants joint set-aside action. Through June, 54 procurements totaling $261, 912 had been restricted to small firms. 

Department of the Interior 

The final agreement for a joint set-aside program between the Small Business Administration and the Department of the Interior was signed on April 12, 1956. Since purchasing by this Department also is decentralized, implementation of the program is being arranged at those Interior field offices whose purchasing volume warrants the program. Through June, only 10 procurements amounting to $44, 975 had been set aside for small business because of the time necessary to implement this program in the field. 

Other Agency Activities at Purchasing Offices 

In addition to reviewing proposed purchases and having suitable ones earmarked for exclusive award to small firms,

Small Business Administration representatives at Government purchasing offices assist small business concerns in many other ways. They offer them advice and assistance in selling to the 

Government; help them obtain opportunities to bid on specific purchases and to get on proper bid lists; assist them in presenting problems to procurement officials; and serve as spokesmen for small business in top-level conferences at the purchasing centers. 

Increasing Small Business Competition 

Another Small Business Administration service to small firms is bringing to their attention items purchased by the Government for which there are few or no small business sources of supply. Small Business Administration representatives assigned to screen procurements at purchasing installations notify the Agency's field offices of items on which the number of bidders is not very great. The field offices then bring the items to the attention of qualified firms which may wish to bid the next time the Government purchases the goods or services. This often increases competition and lowers prices to the Government. 

Small Business Administration field offices also have arranged to supply names of additional small business sources of supply to many procurement installations whose volume of purchasing is not sufficient to warrant establishment of a joint set-aside program. During fiscal year 1956, Small Business Administration field offices furnished names of 7, 265 small business sources of supply to procurement offices in need of additional competition on specified purchases. During the last 6 months, 5, 188 of these were furnished.

Procurement Assistance Counseling Program 

One important aspect of procurement assistance to small firms is counseling them on how to sell to the Government and how to obtain subcontracts.

Agency staff members offer assistance to small firms in determining what items or services they can supply to the Government and which agencies and purchasing offices do the buying. They explain to small business owners how to obtain listing on appropriate bidders lists and how to develop new opportunities to bid. They also offer advice to small business firms regarding procedural problems

on procurements on which they have bid and, if necessary, accompany them in conferences with procurement officials. 

As part of this counseling service, the Small Business Administration also offers advice regarding the following phases of Government procurement: 

1. 2. 

4. 5. 6. 7.

The advantages to qualified small firms of bidding on contracts to develop new items or improve existing ones. 

Bidding on purchases of qualified products. 

Specifications for products or services.

Progress payments or advance payments on contracts.

Handling of claims. 

Supplying performance bonds. 

Becoming registered as a planned wartime materiel supplier. One of the aims of the counseling program is to make available to small firms the fullest possible information concerning Government purchasing. Toward this end, the Agency publishes the "U. S. Government Purchasing Directory," "U. S. Government 

Specifications Directory," and "Pointers on Government Contracting. " 

These publications provide detailed information on who buys what and where in the Federal Government, where Government specifications may be located, and how to go about obtaining prime contracts and subcontracts.

Additional procurement information is provided to small firms by displaying current Government bid sets in field offices and by informing small firm owners of the helpful publications of the 

Small Business Administration and other Government agencies. 

For instance, the International Cooperation Administration provides Small Business Administration field offices with International 

Cooperation Administration Small Business Circulars containing information on hundreds of export trade opportunities for American small businesses under the various International Cooperation 

Administration financed programs. Small Business Administration

field offices use the circulars to furnish interested small manufacturers, suppliers, and exporters descriptions of the needed items, quantities, specifications, shipping instructions and names and addresses of purchasing offices. 

In counseling firms seeking subcontract work, staff members encourage them to list their capabilities in the Small Business Administration facilities inventory and advise them of the advantages of using helpful publications of the Small Business 

Administration and other agencies. Prospective subcontractors also are provided a list of major prime contractors in their areas, the names and addresses of the Atomic Energy Commission's cost-type contractors, information regarding prime contractors doing construction work for the Army Corps of Engineers and the Navy District Public Works Offices, and the addresses of other Government offices in the area which may know of subcontracting opportunities.

For the entire fiscal year 1956, the Small Business Administration field offices reported 21, 785 cases of counseling, and 1, 347 cases of representation to procurement officials in behalf of small firms; 11, 945 counseling cases and 807 cases of representation were reported in the last half of the year. Small 

Business Administration representatives at procurement centers recommended 6, 007 firms to contracting officers for the opportunity to bid on specific purchases, including 3, 231 during the last 6 months. As an example of this latter type of assistance, the Small Business Administration representative at a military installation nominated a small Maine firm for the opportunity to bid on a sole source item on which the previous contractor presumably had necessary tooling, drawings and production know-how. The item was so complex as to require a symposium and bidders' conference. 

Despite these obstacles, the small firm bid on and was awarded the contract at a price more than 30 per cent lower than the Government paid on the previous purchase of the item. Further, it has improved on the required delivery time and has produced equipment of exceptionally high quality.

In another instance a Government purchasing office was considering cancellation of a contract held by a small business defense production pool. The Agency's local office interceded on the pool's behalf and obtained an extension of the delivery date on the contract. Cancellation of the contract would have resulted in a loss of approximately $30, 000 to the pool members.

The following case illustrates counsel and representation by Small Business Administration officials which saved a small firm from bankruptcy and its subcontractors from serious financial distress:

A small Pennsylvania firm asked the Small Business Administration for procurement assistance at a time when (1) it had an unpaid loan of $85, 000 with the Agency; (2) had been placed in default on one military contract with the procurement office taking steps to recover default damages of about $50,000; (3) its invoices on another Government contract were being withheld by the General Accounting Office due to the default action; (4) its request for progress payments made 90 days before on another Government contract had not been granted; and (5) its proposal to produce a large Air Force contract was being rejected because of lack of finances.

Small Business Administration officials took the following steps to assist this firm: (1) Secured a delay in the award of the Air Force contract and a review of the firm's financial status by the contracting officer; (2) suggested a stand-by agreement which the firm's creditors accepted, thereby enabling the firm to finance the Air Force contract; (3) presented the new financial situation to the Air Force, which then awarded the contract to the small firm; (4) obtained agreement from the procurement office which had placed the firm in default to prorate its claim for default damages over a 90-day period, so that the firm could meet its payroll on the Government contract on which it was then producing; (5) obtained the co-operation of the headquarters of the military service involved in the default claim with the result that the default and damage claims by the Government were later dropped; and (6) obtained agreement for progress payments on the small firm's current contract to be expedited.

As a result of the Small Business Administration's assistance, the small firm has been placed in a position where current contracts will enable it to meet its debts and to continue business in a healthy financial condition. 

Prime Contract Referral Program 

An important service to small firms is that of notifying them of Government purchases which are suitable to their facilities until they have obtained listing on the appropriate bidders lists for

the items or services. Small Business Administration field offices review bid sets received from purchasing offices to determine whether firms registered with them can supply the items. Firms whose records indicate that they are potential suppliers are notified of the purchases.

In fiscal year 1956 Small Business Administration field offices made 86, 122 notifications of prime contract opportunities to small business concerns, including 36, 095 notifications in the last 6 months. During this period, the field offices reported that awards totaling $332, 567, 000 had been made to firms which were notified of bidding opportunities by the Agency, including awards totaling $180, 667, 000 in the last half of the fiscal year. It is believed that these figures are much understated since the award information is difficult to obtain.

This type of assistance is illustrated by the action of the Agency's Denver office in referring an Air Force negotiated procurement to a small electronics firm which had requested aid in obtaining Government business. With further advice from the 

Denver staff, the firm prepared its proposal and was awarded a contract amounting to about $160,000.

Subcontracting Program 

Although many small companies hold prime contracts with the Government, subcontracting usually is the most practical way for small firms to share in production of large and highly complex items. Subcontracting also is closer to normal commercial work than is much of Government prime contracting. 

As the basis for effective subcontract assistance, Small Business Administration staff members review carefully the production facilities of a small firm before advising it of potential sources for subcontracts.

Agency representatives contact major prime contractors to learn of their procurement policies and to inquire about items which they subcontract, particularly items for which they want new sources of supply. Small firms which have the necessary production facilities are then referred to those prime contractors in need of additional subcontractors. Because of the complexity of this program, the majority of the work must be accomplished through personal contact by staff members of the Small Business Administration's field offices.

Under the Department of Defense small business subcontracting policy now in operation in the three military departments prime contractors are asked to establish a subcontract program and to make available to the military agencies data on awards to small business. Lists of all contractors which have established such programs have been compiled by the Army, Navy, and Air Force, and have been supplied Small Business Administration field offices for their use in contacting small business liaison officers of these contractors.

During fiscal year 1956, the Small Business Administration referred 3, 508 small firms to prime contractors for possible subcontract work. These referrals resulted from contacts made with 3, 438 contractors to develop subcontract opportunities; 1,806 of these referrals were made in the last 6 months of the fiscal year. 

The results of these efforts cannot be recorded accurately since the Small Business Administration must rely on the voluntary reporting of subcontract awards by the companies. 

One of the highlights of Small Business Administration efforts to assist small subcontractors is the Agency's agreement with the Atomic Energy Commission. That agreement, developed in 1953, has resulted in a close working relationship between the field offices of the two agencies for the purpose of rendering maximum assistance to small business in obtaining subcontracts from Atomic Energy Commission cost-type contractors. Under the arrangement, Atomic Energy Commission cost-type contractors notify Small Business Administration regional offices of subcontract opportunities and the regional offices, in turn, call these to the attention of small firms. In addition, the Small Business Administration has made available to the cost-type contractors its inventories of small plant productive facilities, so that the contractors can select needed small business suppliers in specialized fields. 

The effectiveness of the Atomic Energy Commission's small business program and of the co-operative subcontracting arrangement is illustrated by the upward trend of the small business share of subcontract actions by Atomic Energy Commission costtype prime contractors. This percentage has risen steadily from 

26.7 in fiscal year 1951 to 46. 9 through the first three quarters of fiscal year 1956. 1

Small Business Procurement Conferences 

Such

The Small Business Administration has held conferences in Boston, Tulsa, Dallas, Cincinnati and Baltimore in order to acquaint small business owners and operators with opportunities for selling to the Government and obtaining subcontract work. "clinics" on how to sell to the Government feature speakers from various procurement agencies who explain how small firms may do business with their agencies. In addition, samples of products the 

Government buys regularly are displayed, together with many bid sets, drawings and specifications for items currently being bought by purchasing installations.

An outstanding feature of these conferences is the presence of technically trained personnel to answer questions concerning the various aspects of doing business with the Government as well as to provide counsel on individual problems.

The conferences have met with such enthusiastic response from small firms that it is planned to have the Agency's regional offices conduct small business procurement conferences in the principal cities in their regions during fiscal year 1957. 

Army Ordnance officials have reported that at least 20 Ordnance contracts were placed with small firms in the Tulsa area as a direct result of the firms' participation in the procurement conference there in March.

Small Business Administration Prime Contract Program 

The Small Business Administration is empowered to enter into contracts with any Department or Agency of the United States Government to furnish supplies and services, and to arrange for the performance of such contracts through subcontracting to small business concerns or others, as may be necessary. 

The Small Business Administration holds the opinion that its authority to take prime contracts in order to extend subcontracting should continue on a stand-by basis for use in emergencies.

CHAPTER V PRODUCTION AND MANAGEMENT ASSISTANCE 

During the last 6-months period, the Small Business Administration provided small firms many types of production and management assistance: 1. 

It assisted small businesses in obtaining materials and equipment. 2. 

It worked to insure for small business a fair share of the benefits of accelerated tax amortization. 3.

It gave advice and assistance in the formation of small business production pools. 

4. It continued to maintain the small business facilities inventory. 5.

It gave technical production assistance to individual small business concerns to help solve their problems. 6.

It made available to small concerns information on Government packing and packaging specifications to assist them in selling their services and products to the Government. 

7. It helped small firms obtain technical information and data on product research and development problems. 8. 

It granted certificates of competency enabling small firms to obtain Government prime contracts which otherwise would have been denied them. 73

9. It continued its program of helpful publications for small business concerns, including Management, Technical, and 

Small Marketers Aids and Management booklets. 

10. It cosponsored administrative management courses for small business owners and managers in co-operation with 57 universities and other educational institutions throughout the Nation.

Materials and Equipment Assistance 

During the last 6 months most basic materials and types of production equipment were in adequate supply to meet the needs of small firms. The aluminum supply-demand situation, which was particularly acute during the last 6 months of 1955, eased substantially due to the increase in supply resulting from new or enlarged facilities for producing primary aluminum ingots. However, the high level of industrial activity created a heavy demand for some products making it impossible for many small concerns to obtain the increased supplies necessary to assure their economic growth and a profitable return on their investment. The most serious shortage affecting small firms during the last 6 months appeared to be nickel, and the limitation in the supply, as well as the increased requirements of the Department of Defense, made it virtually impossible to assist small firms, especially those not engaged in defense production. 

Other shortages appeared in the supply of steel, especially plates and fabricated shapes, iron and steel scrap, cement, paper, industrial diamonds and selenium. As in the previous 

6 months, many firms found their suppliers limiting shipments to a percentage of an established historical purchasing pattern. 

Consequently, newly established small firms found it difficult or impossible to obtain scarce materials. The Small Business 

Administration made continuing efforts to locate materials for small firms.

The production of machine tools was adequate in most cases, although there was a shortage of specialized types. The 

Small Business Administration regularly provides assistance to

small concerns in locating commercial sources of used equipment or surplus Government-owned equipment. Moreover, all Small 

Business Administration field offices screen proposed sales of surplus Government-owned equipment to locate specific types sought by small firms which have been unable to locate comparable new equipment.

The Agency acts as spokesman for small concerns before other Government agencies which are considering the adoption of materials and equipment policies or programs affecting small businesses. In carrying out this function, the Small 

Business Administration recently has participated in industry conferences and meetings dealing with the following subjects: 

Use of reinforced plastics for packing and materials handling; prime aluminum products; titanium production and fabrication; aluminum smelters; and steel forms and reports.

The Agency also reviews proposed new regulations, or changes in existing regulations, dealing with materials and equipment in order to protect the interests of small business concerns. Information on such regulations is provided to small firms through the Small Business Administration field offices. 

Tax Amortization Assistance 

The Government's accelerated tax amortization program is an incentive in the form of tax relief for firms to expand defense production facilities. The Small Business Administration helps small firms obtain a fair share of the tax write-off privileges through representation on the interagency Ad Hoc Committee on Expansion Goals, which studies the need for requesting a "small business share" of each proposed new expansion goal. In addition, when necessary, the Agency intercedes for small firms before the Office of Defense Mobilization and other Government agencies to which the Office of Defense Mobilization may refer small business applications for accelerated tax amortization privileges. At the request of the Office of Defense Mobilization or other agencies, Small Business Administration field offices obtain additional data pertaining to specific small business applications. Often, the additional information results in approval of the applications. In addition, the Agency frequently is asked by small concerns to determine the status of their applications.

During the last 6 months the number of open expansion goals was reduced from 34 to 30, and most open goals are for facilities requiring too great a capital expenditure for the typical small firm.

However, although the number of cases handled by the Small Business Administration was small, the value of applications approved exceeded the previous 6 months by $220,000.

281, 276 4,641, 743 84 13 The Small Business Administration gives advice and assistance in the formation of small business production pools, and formally approves their proposed operations in accordance with section 217 of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended. 

This approval is subject to the concurrence of the Attorney 

General and the Chairman of the Federal Trade Commission. 

Production pools consist primarily of groups of small concerns voluntarily associated together to obtain and perform jointly contracts for the production of articles, equipment,

supplies and materials, or for the furnishing of services, needed for the defense effort. The firms have a mutual agreement as to their organization, relationship and procedure. 

During the last 6-months period, the Small Business Administration approved the voluntary programs of two production pools, the Huntington Production Pool, Huntington, West Virginia, and American Scientific and Technical Research Organization, Inc. (ASTRO, Inc.), Detroit, Michigan. In addition, applications from four companies seeking to take part in established pools were received and approved by the Agency. 

Increased interest in the formation of pools is indicated also by the receipt of 75 inquiries pertaining to pools by Small Business Administration field offices, in addition to a number of inquiries received by the Washington office.

At present, there are 6 approved small business production pools, composed of 51 companies employing approximately 3,000 persons. Twenty-one of these companies, with more than 2,000 employees, helped organize new pools or joined established ones, during the first 6 months of 1956. 

Through June 30, 1956, 2 of the presently active pools had reported 229 prime contract awards amounting to more than $946, 351, in addition to 4 subcontract awards amounting to $85, 976. A third pool has reported receiving negotiated prime contracts valued at approximately $4, 000, 000 in connection with a Department of the Navy program. The other three pools received no prime contracts or subcontracts during the 6-months period.

Inventory of Small Plant Facilities 

Each regional office of the Small Business Administration registers small companies in its are in an inventory of small plant productive facilities. Registration in the inventory is entirely voluntary for small firms. The small business owner who wishes his firm registered completes a questionnaire giving essential data on his company.

The Small Business Administration field office staff reviews the information and codifies it on the basis of the Standard Industrial Classification. 

This roster of registered small concerns is especially important for purposes of mobilization planning and industrial potential. Present uses of the facility records include (1) helping 398407 56-6

the registered companies obtain Government contracts; (2) making additional bidders available to Government procurement agencies; and (3) locating firms which have special types of machinery and equipment. 

As of June 30, 1956, there were 32, 427 small companies registered with the Agency.

Production Assistance 

Small plant operators frequently are confronted with technical and production problems for which outside counsel is needed. The Small Business Administration provides such assistance through regional office staff members with professional backgrounds in engineering, production and related fields. 

For example, the staff members answer questions relating to conversion of existing facilities for defense production work, acquisition of new equipment, plant layout, methods engineering, and resumption of operations following a disaster.

If a regional production or industrial specialist finds that a small company's problems are of sufficient scope or complexity, he advises it to retain professional consultants. 

The Agency is careful to avoid entering into competition with the professional field.

Here are a few examples of the types of small firm problems with which help is given: 

1. A small California firm which manufactures canvas items had difficulty obtaining Government contracts because of poor management and lack of quality control. Under the Small 

Business Administration's guidance the firm improved its operations and quality control. As a result its Government orders have increased to the point that the firm now has four times as many employees as it did 2 years ago. 2. Soon after it opened a new department in the field of light metal stampings, a Texas chemical products firm found that it lacked necessary technical knowledge in many phases of the operation. A Small Business Administration representative analyzed the firm's problems and located a professional engineering consultant who agreed to work with the firm on a part-time basis.

3. The operator of a small welding shop in Minnesota developed a rough design of a truck tank for delivery of turkey mash directly from the mill to the feeder. However, he was confronted by design and production obstacles. A Small Business 

Administration industrial specialist suggested changes in design to simplify production and methods for fabricating parts and assemblies to effect cost savings. 

4. A group of persons who wished to form a company to manufacture railroad signal flares called upon the Small 

Business Administration for assistance. Agency representatives aided in obtaining a suitable location for the plant and in getting the necessary equipment and supplies. 

5. A Washington firm developed and patented a highspeed automatic wrapping machine to wrap and seal meats, vegetables and bakery goods with cellophane, pliofilm, polyethylene and similar materials. At the request of the manufacturer a Small Business Administration production specialist studied the machine and recommended that certain refinements be incorporated in the production units. The Agency staff member suggested also that assembly jigs and fixtures be designed and estimates of assembled unit production costs be obtained. The management has now completed the production assembly jigs and fixtures and has under production the first of its machines. 

6. A plastic molding and tool and die company in 

Oregon had been experiencing heavy financial losses due to a high percentage of rejected parts produced from various dies. 

After study of the firm's production techniques, a Small Business Administration production specialist recommended that improved temperature control be adhered to in the casting cycle of the molding machines and that greater circulation of coolants adjacent to the cavities in the die blocks be provided where necessary. 

The firm is now exercising greater production and quality control and operating on a profitable basis. 

Specifications Assistance 

One of the continuing problems of small business concerns, especially those bidding on Government purchases for the first time, is how to obtain copies of necessary specifications. 

In order to help small concerns obtain increased information on

specifications, the Small Business Administration has prepared the "U. S. Government Specifications Directory," which shows the location of over 500 places throughout the country where small business owners may refer to Government specifications and indexes. The directory also discusses the purchasing specifications used by the military and civilian agencies of the Government and provides related information. 

Another Small Business Administration service for small concerns is a set of packaging and packing specifications available for reference purposes at all Agency field offices. 

These sets contain approximately 380 of the packaging and packing specifications most often required by small business concerns in selling their products and services to the Government. The 

Small Business Administration field offices also maintain files of Government specifications compiled from expired bid sets, and make these limited stocks available to small firms in need of them.

Another function of the Small Business Administration is to review proposed new or interim Federal specifications to make certain that none of their provisions would restrict bidding opportunities for small concerns. The Agency reviewed 171 specifications sent to it by the General Services Administration during the period January 1 through June 30, 1956, and in a number of instances recommended deletion of possible restrictive or discriminatory provisions. 

Products Assistance Service The Small Business Administration's Products Assistance 

Service is designed to help small concerns gain access to needed product development information available from Government and industry. Since start of the service in July 1954, requests for this type of assistance have increased steadily. 

As part of this service the Agency 1. Helps small concerns gain access to data on research and on product, process and market development needed in solving day-to-day operating problems, and in planning production and marketing programs.

1 2. Provides small firms a clearinghouse for information on available products and processes which can be used to maintain, diversify, or expand their operations. 

The Small Business Administration does not maintain laboratory or staff facilities for research on product development, nor does it perform marketing research services. Instead, it enlists the co-operation of industry, Government agencies and Departments, research organizations, trade associations, and educational institutions in solving problems presented to it by small firms.

The Small Business Administration also helps small concerns gain access to statistical information needed in planning marketing programs. While it is obvious that the most complete information can be gained by a thorough market survey conducted by professional organizations providing this type of service, publicly available information in many instances adequately fills the need.

During the last 6 months, 2, 185 firms requested and were furnished information on various phases of product, process and market development. Since the service was established in July 1954, approximately 8, 700 firms have been assisted. 

The clearinghouse feature of the service is conducted by means of a Products List Circular. Issued monthly, the circular lists abstracts of new and improved products and processes which have been made available for production and commercial distribution by inventors, patent attorneys, private firms, Government agencies, and research and product development organizations. Thousands of small concerns are now receiving the Products List Circular regularly. During this reporting period, 801 firms requested and received information on the availability of 1,571 items listed in the circular which they were interested in producing and marketing. 

In addition, the Agency is making available to small firms information it has developed on more than 10, 000 patents released by the American Telephone and Telegraph Company and the International Business Machines Corporation for license manufacturing on a no-cost basis.

The Agency also has published recently booklets entitled "Developing and Selling New Products" (published jointly with the 

U. S. Department of Commerce), "New Product Introduction for 

Small Business Owners," and "Technology and New Products. "

These publications are designed to cover a wide range of management and production problems encountered in product, process, and market development.

Certificates of Competency 

A certificate of competency is a statement from the 

Administrator of the Small Business Administration to the contract ing officer of a Government procurement agency certifying that the small company named therein is qualified to perform a specific contract on which it has submitted a bid. The certification program provides an appeal procedure for a small concern whose bid has been rejected by a procurement agency because it questions the firm's ability to perform the contract.

The Small Business Administration's decision on an application for a certificate is based on a careful survey of the applicant's production, technical, and financial capabilities. 

After a contract has been awarded as a result of issuance of a certificate of competency, the Small Business Administration maintains a continuing interest during the entire life of the contract. 

If the contractor has difficulty in meeting production schedules because of technical or other problems the Agency offers assistance in solving them.

Managerial Assistance 

The Small Business Administration provides managerial assistance to small firms by means of management publications, management education, and management counseling. 

Management Publications 

To provide businessmen with practical information on ways of improving their management skills, the Small Business Administration has issued publications covering 148 individual business subjects. (See appendix J, page 190, for a complete listing of these.) Fifty-two of these publications may now be obtained inexpensively in bound form for permanent reference purposes.

Management Aids for Small Manufacturers 

To date, 74 of these 4-page, free leaflets have been issued. Recent publications include: "Planning Your Working 

Capital Requirements, " "Making a Marketing Survey," "Broadening Your Manufacturing Opportunities," and "Office Mechanization for Small Industry." As of June 30, 1956, 1, 710, 845 copies of the Management Aids had been distributed, of which 155,970 copies had been distributed during the last 6 months.

Small Marketers Aids 

Increased emphasis is currently being given to this series of publications as a means of providing managerial assistance to owners and managers of small retail, wholesale and service concerns. This series is designed to fill gaps in existing business literature, which deals chiefly with techniques suitable to larger enterprises. To date, 14 of these short, free

leaflets have been issued. Typical recent subjects include, "Sizing Up Small Business Locations," "Sales Training for 

Small Wholesalers," and "Diversification for Small Marketers."

Total distribution of Marketers Aids now stands at 260, 665 

copies, of which 119,000 copies were distributed during the last 6 months.

Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers 

To date, 43 of these leaflets have been issued. The subject matter of the individual Aids extends over a wide range of production processes, materials, equipment, and maintenance problems. Representative recent Aids include: "Plastics 

Opportunities for Small Business," and "How Good Plant

Housekeeping Will Increase Production." Through June 30, 1956, the Agency had distributed 533, 050 copies of the Aids, including 

53, 405 copies during the last 6 months. 

Small Business Management Series 

When a management subject cannot be adequately covered in a short leaflet, the Small Business Administration has a leading authority in the field prepare a manuscript on the subject. There are 17 publications available at reasonable prices from the 

Superintendent of Documents. 

Continuing use of these publications by owners and managers of small concerns is indicated by total sales to date of more than 390,000 copies, with 40,000 copies having been sold during the last 6 months. The following booklets are the best sellers: "Cost Accounting for Small Manufacturers," with sales of 37, 405 copies; "Cutting Office Costs in Small Plants," with sales of 36, 585 copies; "Human Relations in Small Industry, with sales of 32, 944 copies; and "A Handbook of Small Business 

Finance," with sales of 29,664 copies. 11

Management Education 

The program of cosponsoring short courses in administrative management for small business owners and managers continued to thrive during the first half of 1956. These courses, designed to help the small business executive improve his knowledge of good management practices, are conducted co-operatively by the Small Business Administration and educational institutions throughout the Nation. The Small Business Administration encourages the organization of the courses and assists as cosponsor by acting as a clearinghouse for ideas concerning subject matter, teaching methods and administrative techniques. The educational institutions assume the educational and financial responsibility for the courses. 

Costs are defrayed by tuition fees paid by the businessmen who take the course. 

Since January 1, 1956, 53 more courses have been completed in 36 educational institutions. This ends the second full school year of the program. In the first year (September 1954 through June 1955) 54 courses were conducted, while this last year (September 1955 through June 1956) 99 were completed increase of 83 per cent. Courses were conducted in 40 and 57 educational institutions, respectively. Including the two pilot an 

---

courses offered in the spring of 1954, a total of 155 courses have been completed in 69 different educational institutions since start of the program. Most institutions repeat the courses once they have begun to offer them. Approximately 4, 400 businessmen have attended the courses. 

To encourage further these courses, the Small Business Administration is working with the Society for Advancement of Management toward the goal of having local Society for Advancement of Management chapters initiate and organize courses.

Lending Operations

This balance sheet is predicated upon the records of the 

Small Business Administration as of June 30, 1956 and prior to audit. It covers only the regular lending operations of the Agency, conducted under the provisions of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended. The operations with respect to the liquidation of the Reconstruction Finance Corporation disaster loans, transferred under the provisions of the Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1954, are described in separate exhibits. 

Footnotes to Statement of Income and Expense 

Liabilities exclude loan commitments of $36, 153, 737. 02 as of June 30, 1956, the details of which are shown on the accompanying statement, "Status of Revolving Fund. " 

Lending Operations

Covers only the regular lending operations of the Small 

Business Administration. Excludes $2, 125, 415. 86, the net administrative costs of other programs provided for in the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended. Also excludes $562, 441. 97, the net administrative costs of operations with respect to the liquidation of Reconstruction Finance Corporation disaster loans, transferred under the provisions of Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1954, which are described in separate exhibits. 

This provision has been made to accumulate reserves for possible losses, based upon Government lending agency experience factors developed over a period of years, applicable to loan disbursements under the various programs.
